> The user is asking two conceptual questions about the geometry processing pipeline. First, they want to understand why the chain-based fallback mechanism is necessary at all, and second, they're questioning whether stage 3.4's abortion is related to the topological structure of the surface — specifically whether it's dealing with something like a cylinder with a hole punched through it. I'm starting to explain the first question by noting that stage 3.3 computes edge curves using surface-surface intersections of the fitted surfaces adjacent to each edge.
> 
> The core issue is that fitted surfaces are approximations derived from mesh data, and they often fail to intersect cleanly where the mesh indicates a junction should be. This is especially problematic at tangent junctions — like where a fillet meets a plane — where the surfaces meet at nearly zero dihedral angle. Numerically, the two fitted surfaces might miss each other entirely if one is approximated slightly smaller, or they might interpenetrate and create two nearby curves if one is slightly larger. The intersection algorithm itself is also unreliable: it can fail to find any curves, return partial arcs when curves cross UV poles, place curves in wrong locations, or crash entirely on degenerate inputs. That's why tangent edges get special analytic constructions, and why even those sometimes fail — necessitating the fallback chain as a last resort.
> 
> The mesh boundary chain — the actual polyline of mesh vertices along the border between the two faces' regions — serves as ground truth because those vertices lie on the true junction within the mesh's tolerance, regardless of how well the surfaces were fitted. I can use this chain both as a validator to check how far the computed intersection deviates from reality, and as a curve source when everything else fails: fit a circle or B-spline through the chain vertices. The trade-off is that a chain-fit curve is only as smooth and accurate as the mesh sampling allows, and it won't lie exactly on either fitted surface — but the pcurve projection step can absorb that discrepancy.
> 
> On 3DBenchy, 684 out of 68,924 edges fell back to using the chain because the analytic intersection failed. That's because Benchy is heavily freeform with 32,000 surfaces — lots of small fitted B-spline patches and tangent junctions everywhere — so the surface-based intersection machinery hits its failure modes frequently. The reason I don't just use the chain always is that analytic curves are exact geometry: smooth, lying on both surfaces, independent of mesh resolution. The chain is jagged but reliable. I want the exact solution when it works and the ground truth when it doesn't.
> 
> Now the user is asking whether face 5841 is topologically just a surface with 3 holes — like a cylinder with another surface punching through it. The key distinction here is between plain holes (simple loops) and winding loops.
> 
> The limit isn't on how many boundary loops a face can have — it's on winding loops, which are loops whose net winding number around the surface's periodic direction is ±1. A hole punched in a cylinder doesn't wind around the axis; it's a contractible loop in UV space with winding 0, so it's a plain loop and any number of those are fine. A winding loop, by contrast, goes all the way around the periodic direction like the rim of a cylindrical band, crosses the UV seam, and requires the face to have a seam edge that the seam planner handles.
> 
> The valid cases are 0 winding loops (a patch), 2 winding loops (a band with top and bottom rims connected by a seam edge), or 1 (a pole cap like a cone apex with one rim plus the pole). Three winding loops is topologically impossible for a connected region because on a cylinder, each winding loop separates the surface into two pieces — if three disjoint loops each wrap around, the region between them would fragment into disconnected components, making the middle loop interior rather than boundary. So a connected face can have at most 2 winding boundary loops, plus any number of contractible holes.
> 
> Face 5841's boundary loops are scattered across three far-apart regions of the model, which means the "face" isn't actually a connected surface at all. The stage-2 hypothesis suggests one fitted surface claimed mesh faces in three or more disconnected patches — places where the same analytic surface (like a plane or cylinder) happens to fit locally in separate areas, such as coaxial cylinders at the same radius or a large sphere grazing multiple regions. Each disconnected patch contributes its own boundary loops, and two of them form closed loops that wind in this face's UV chart.
> 
> Now I'm reconsidering the relationship between winding count and disconnected regions. The two remote closed-loop edges are junction curves made periodic, and in the face's UV chart, a closed loop around a distant region of the same surface would have zero winding if it doesn't encircle the axis (like on a small-radius cylinder). For the winding to be ±1, the loop must wrap around the periodic direction. The loops at those two distant locations each show winding behavior, which suggests the surface might be a small-radius cylinder or periodic band where a closed junction loop elsewhere on the model maps around the period.
> 
> The most likely explanation is that the face has one legitimate pair of winding loops forming a band, plus one additional winding loop from a disconnected region. Either way, the disconnected regions are the real issue here, supported by the 3D positions. The fundamental problem is that a face bounded by three winding loops can't exist as a single connected surface region in B-Rep terms — the model actually has separate faces there, so I'd need to split the mesh region into connected components and generate one face descriptor for each.
> 
> On the "at most 2" constraint: one winding loop is fine (a cap with a rim and degenerate pole edge), two is fine (a band), anything more is an error. A cylinder with a hole works in this architecture because the hole loop has zero winding and becomes an inner wire, while the outer boundary consists of two winding rims plus a seam edge forming a composite outer wire. ## 🤖 Assistant
<!-- msg_011Cd6aktgG5k9Qk7PexQKaN -->

## Why the chain-based fallback exists

Stage 3.3's primary method for building an edge curve is exact geometry: intersect the two fitted surfaces adjacent to the edge (`GeomAPI_IntSS`), or construct the curve analytically for tangent pairs. When it works, this is ideal — the curve is smooth, lies exactly on both surfaces, and is independent of mesh resolution.

But both inputs to that computation are *fitted approximations*, and the junction is exactly where their errors collide:

- **Tangent junctions are numerically ill-posed.** Where a fillet meets a plane, the true surfaces touch at zero dihedral angle. Fit the fillet's radius a hair small and the fitted surfaces never touch (`nb_lines=0`); a hair large and they interpenetrate in two curves near the true tangency line. On Benchy, thousands of edges are tangent (44,803 classified tangent), and the analytic constructions for them have their own preconditions that sometimes fail.
- **IntSS itself is fallible on freeform surfaces.** Intersections involving B-spline patches are computed by approximation: it can return nothing, return partial arcs (a circle through a sphere's UV poles comes back as two disconnected arcs), return a curve parameterized strangely, or — as we saw at edge 68,124 — throw a C++ exception outright.
- **Even a "successful" intersection can be in the wrong place.** The intersection of two slightly-wrong surfaces is a slightly-wrong curve; the observed case in the TODO was a curve 0.402 mm from where the mesh says the junction is.

The mesh boundary chain — the polyline of actual mesh vertices along the border between the two faces' triangle regions — is the one representation of the junction that *always exists and is always in the right place*, because it comes straight from the input mesh rather than from fitted geometry. Its accuracy is only chordal-tolerance-level and it lies on neither fitted surface exactly, so it's a fallback, not the default. The pipeline uses it two ways: as ground truth to *validate* every constructed curve (deviation check → repair), and as a curve source of last resort (fit a circle or B-spline through the chain). On Benchy, 684 of 68,924 edges (~1%) ended up chain-fitted — the price of a model that's mostly freeform patches and tangencies rather than crisp analytic intersections.

## Why stage 3.4 aborts — and no, it's not the 3-holes case

The distinction that matters is **plain loops vs. winding loops**, and the limit is only on the latter.

A hole punched in a cylindrical face is a *plain* loop: in the cylinder's UV space it's a contractible closed curve with winding number 0 around the periodic direction. Any number of those is supported — each becomes an inner wire of the face. Your cylinder-with-a-hole example builds fine.

A *winding* loop wraps all the way around the periodic direction — think of the top and bottom rims of a soda-can label. Such loops cross the UV seam, so the face needs a seam edge, and the seam planner handles the topologically possible configurations: 0 winding loops (ordinary patch), 1 (a cap whose other "end" is a pole, like a cone apex region), or 2 (a band: top rim + bottom rim, seam edge connecting them).

Three winding loops is not a more complicated face — it's an impossible one, and that's why it's a hard error rather than an unimplemented feature. On a cylinder, every loop that wraps around the axis *separates* the surface into two halves. If three disjoint wrapping loops all bound one region, then the middle loop would have material on both of its sides — making it an interior curve of the region, not a boundary. A connected region of a periodic surface can have at most two winding boundary loops, plus arbitrarily many holes.

So when `scan_face` counts three, it's telling us face 5841 isn't a connected region at all. The log evidence confirms it: most of the face's boundary edges cluster near [10.7, 15.1, 19.7], but its two closed-loop boundary edges sit at [20.6, 12.9, 26.3] and [−15.5, 20.5, 3.9] — three mutually distant parts of the model. What happened is upstream: stage 2 fitted one surface and its region-growing/selection claimed mesh faces in (at least) three *disconnected* patches of the mesh that all happen to lie on that same surface — the classic case being one infinite cylinder or plane that several separate features of the model graze. Each disconnected patch contributes its own boundary loops; here two of the remote patches are full rings around the periodic direction, so they classify as winding, and 1 + 2 = 3.

That's why the fix I recorded points at hypothesis integrity rather than the seam planner: the correct output for a hypothesis spanning three disconnected mesh regions is three separate B-Rep faces (one per connected component), each with a sane 0/1/2-winding boundary. Extending the seam planner to "support" three winding loops would be building machinery for a face that can't exist.

930	/// Distance from a mesh vertex to a selected surface's fitted geometry.
931	fn vertex_distance_to_selected(
932	    v: &MeshVertex,
933	    surface: &SelectedSurface,
934	    planar_hypotheses: &[PlanarHypothesis],
935	    conical_hypotheses: &[ConicalHypothesis],
936	    spherical_hypotheses: &[SphericalHypothesis],
937	    toroidal_hypotheses: &[ToroidalHypothesis],
938	    bspline_hypotheses: &[BSplineHypothesis],
939	) -> f64 {
940	    match surface {
941	        SelectedSurface::BSpline(i) => {
942	            bspline_hypotheses[*i].distance(&[v.x, v.y, v.z])
943	        }
944	        SelectedSurface::Planar(i) => {
945	            let h = &planar_hypotheses[*i];
946	            (h.normal[0] * v.x + h.normal[1] * v.y + h.normal[2] * v.z - h.distance).abs()
947	        }
948	        SelectedSurface::Conical(i) => {
949	            let h = &conical_hypotheses[*i];
950	            if h.is_cylinder() {
951	                cylindrical::vertex_to_cylinder_distance(
952	                    v, &h.apex, &h.axis_direction, h.radius_at_apex,
953	                ).abs()
954	            } else {
955	                conical::vertex_to_cone_distance(
956	                    v, &h.apex, &h.axis_direction, h.half_angle,
957	                ).abs()
958	            }
959	        }
960	        SelectedSurface::Spherical(i) => {
961	            let h = &spherical_hypotheses[*i];
962	            spherical::vertex_to_sphere_distance(v, &h.center, h.radius).abs()
963	        }
964	        SelectedSurface::Toroidal(i) => {
965	            let h = &toroidal_hypotheses[*i];
966	            toroidal::vertex_to_torus_distance(
967	                v, &h.center, &h.axis_direction, h.major_radius, h.minor_radius,
968	            ).abs()
969	        }
970	    }
971	}

973	/// Refine surface assignments at region boundaries (DEVELOPMENT_PLAN §2.6
974	/// "Surface refitting"). BFS greedily absorbs border faces that fit within
975	/// tolerance but belong more naturally to the adjacent surface — e.g. a face
976	/// one row past a fillet junction absorbed by the plane. The misassignment
977	/// shifts the mesh region boundary by a row, which shifts reconstructed edge
978	/// boundary vertices and corner vertices off the true feature curves.
979	///
980	/// For each face on a region boundary, compare the max vertex distance to
981	/// its own surface against each adjacent selected surface, and move the face
982	/// when an adjacent surface fits at least 2× better. The margin prevents
983	/// oscillation for true junction faces (whose vertices lie on both surfaces
984	/// equally). Iterates to a fixpoint (boundaries only ever move locally).
985	/// Hypothesis geometry is NOT re-fitted: the fits were validated during BFS;
986	/// only the region map (face membership) changes.

989	    mesh: &ConnectedMesh,

## 🤖💭 Assistant
<!-- msg_011Cd6bpHnmaHbaGy34zoDTA -->

> I found the issue in the greedy selection step of `select_surfaces` — when a candidate wins, it filters out faces that were already claimed by earlier winners, but it doesn't recheck whether the remaining faces still form a connected component. This means a winning candidate could end up with disconnected face fragments if an earlier winner severed its connectivity.
> 
> The same problem appears in `refine_boundary_assignments`, which moves individual border faces based on fit ratio and can sever thin bridges that connect regions. And I need to check whether `absorb_redundant_surfaces` requires adjacency when absorbing one surface into another.


## 🤖💭 Assistant
<!-- msg_011Cd6bpHnmaHbaGy34zoDTA -->

> I found the first mechanism in `select_surfaces` at line 700 where the winner keeps only its unassigned faces without rechecking connectivity, overwriting the hypothesis's face list. Now I'll check the absorption pass.

## 🤖 Assistant
<!-- msg_011Cd6c9Nan4TfFQFecaf1q8 -->

Your intuition is right — and wrong in an interesting place. BFS *does* guarantee every hypothesis is edge-connected **at deduction time**. Connectivity dies later, and for face 5841 the evidence points at something subtler than I originally claimed. Two findings:

## 1. Three passes rewrite face membership after BFS, none re-checks connectivity

- **Greedy selection (stage 2.6) — the structural hole.** Hypotheses of different types overlap freely (a face can carry planar + conical + toroidal candidates). Selection repeatedly picks the largest-remaining-area candidate and gives it only its **still-unassigned** faces ([mod.rs:700](src/stage2/mod.rs:700)), then *overwrites* the hypothesis's face list with that remnant ([mod.rs:874](src/stage2/mod.rs:874)). If an earlier, bigger winner claimed a chunk out of the middle of a region, the remnant is disconnected — silently.
- **Boundary refinement** ([mod.rs:988](src/stage2/mod.rs:988)) moves individual border faces to an adjacent surface that fits ≥2× better. Fillet strips like this one are 1–2 faces wide, so stealing one face can sever the strip.
- **Absorption** requires the source to be mesh-adjacent to the target, so each merge preserves connectivity — but it happily merges *into* an already-disconnected target, propagating the damage.

## 2. But face 5841's log evidence tells a stranger story

I checked the numbers. The fitted surface is a cylinder of radius **0.2048 mm**. The ordinary boundary edges cluster at axial station +16.4; the two remote closed loops are at stations +28.5 and −14.7 — and all three sites lie *on* the cylinder (radial distances 0.19–0.21 mm), spanning 43 mm along one straight line.

Then the kicker: faces 9597 and 9598 — the far ends of those closed-loop edges — are **single-facet islands**: 1 edge, 1 neighbor (5841), 0 vertices. Each is one leftover mesh facet completely surrounded by 5841's region, exactly what selection's step-4 single-face-planar fallback produces. So region 5841 doesn't merely *reach* three distant places — at two of them it fully encircles a lone stray facet.

And their edge curves are full 2π circles (parameter spans exactly 6.2832) that passed chain validation *without repair* — vacuously. The feature's entire diameter is 0.41 mm, essentially equal to `surface_tolerance` (0.4 mm): every point near a station of this hair-thin feature is "within tolerance" of the full ring, so the deviation check cannot distinguish a small contractible loop around one island facet from a ring wrapping the whole rod. In UV, a full-circle curve winds by construction — so those two "winding loops" may be an artifact of the curves, not the topology.

## What this means

Two candidate root causes, not mutually exclusive:

- **(a) Genuine disconnection** via selection remnants / refinement — the mechanism you suspected couldn't exist does exist, one level above BFS.
- **(b) Sub-tolerance feature scale**: BFS growth along a 0.2 mm rounded edge is barely constrained (every vertex of a feature thinner than the tolerance fits *any* surface threading it), the region may even be a connected 43 mm snake, and the bogus full-circle island-junction curves then manufacture winding loops.

The cheap discriminator is a debug dump that runs one BFS over each selected hypothesis's *final* face list and reports component counts — that immediately says whether 5841 is disconnected (a) or connected-but-misclassified (b). The fix differs accordingly: connected-component splitting for (a), a minimum-feature-size gate (and/or a scale-aware chain check) for (b).

I've rewritten the TODO.md Active entry with this refined diagnosis and the discriminating next step — uncommitted for now since this was an exploration pass; say the word and I'll commit it.
